score (\(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)) with 95\% bootstrap interval and
per-cohort points. Right: paired difference (\texttt{method\ −\ PCA})
across the 12 cohorts, where solid colored intervals exclude zero.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{figure}[htbp]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{03_experiments_files/figure-pdf/fig-three-probes-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-three-probes}Coordinate-aligned SiMR models preserve
single-axis Random Forest accuracy while dense PCA drops sharply due to
coordinate mixing (\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_benchmark}, mean \(\pm\) SE
over cohort blocks at \(q = 0.5\)). Grouped bars compare Subspace
Fidelity (\texttt{Ridge(XV)}, blue), Single-Axis Interpretability
(\texttt{RandomForest(XV)}, orange), and Shared Patient Consensus
(\texttt{Ridge(U)}, green).}

\end{figure}%

Three empirical principles emerge from
Table~\ref{tbl-global-leaderboard} and Figures
\ref{fig-cd-diagram}--\ref{fig-three-probes}:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Multi-View Alignment Against Single-Matrix Factorization}: The
  leading mean Friedman ranks on the linear entrypoint probe are
  \texttt{NED} (3.00), \texttt{NEDPP} (3.33), \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}
  (3.58), \texttt{LEND} (3.58), against \texttt{PCA} (5.38) and
  \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} (5.42) (Figure~\ref{fig-benchmark-uncertainty}).
  On heterogeneous cohorts such as \texttt{mfeat}
  (\(p = [76, 64, 6, 47]\)) and \texttt{Shared+Private}
  (\(p = [160, 60, 30]\)), concatenated \texttt{PCA} reaches a linear
  score of 0.727 (\texttt{mfeat} accuracy) and 0.291
  (\texttt{Shared+Private} \(R^2\)), against 0.973 (\texttt{NED}) and
  0.949 (\texttt{SiMLR}) for the best multi-view model. Over the 12
  datasets (seeds averaged, Wilcoxon signed-rank against \texttt{PCA}
  with Holm adjustment, Appendix~\ref{sec-app-operational}), the linear
  score differs from \texttt{PCA} at \(p < 0.05\) for 0 of 6 non-PCA
  models.
\item
  \textbf{Coordinate Alignment and the Random Forest Axis Drop}: As
  established in Equation~\ref{eq-subspace-invariance}, ridge regression
  on \(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) is insensitive to orthogonal or
  oblique column mixing \(\mathbf{V}_m \mathbf{M}\), hiding the fact
  that dense \texttt{PCA} mixes distinct mechanisms across columns. With
  coordinate-splitting decision trees (\texttt{RandomForest}),
  \texttt{PCA} loses -0.044 on average across the 12 cohorts (Random
  Forest minus linear score), falling from 0.125 to -0.000 on the weak
  sparse factors of \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak} and from 0.148 to 0.042 on
  \texttt{ADNI-MCI} (72\% lower). The smallest drops are \texttt{SiMLR}
  (-0.017), \texttt{NEDPP} (-0.020).
\item
  \textbf{Operating Points and Feature Density (\(q = 0.5\)
  vs.~\(q = 0.8\))}: Figure~\ref{fig-operating-point-and-support} shows
  the quantile threshold \(q\) as a lens on feature support recovery
  without conflating scales on secondary axes. Across the 756
  default-positivity fits, the mean linear score is 0.713, 0.716 and
  0.715 at \(q = \text{None}\), \(0.5\) and \(0.8\), and the mean Random
  Forest axis score is 0.687, 0.689 and 0.688; at \(q = 0.8\), 57.8\% of
  loadings are zero while 99.8\% of the best linear score is retained.
  At \(q = 0.5\), support recovery on the \(50\%\)-sparse
  \texttt{Linear} regime ranges from \(F_1 =\) 0.500 (\texttt{PCA}) to
  1.000 (\texttt{NSAFlow-PCA}), and reaches 0.875 on
  \texttt{NonnegParts} (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}). On the focal
  \(20\%\)-active modules, the best \(F_1\) at \(q = 0.8\) is 0.831 on
  \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak} (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}) and 0.660 on
  \texttt{Shared+Private} (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[htbp]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{03_experiments_files/figure-pdf/fig-operating-point-and-support-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-operating-point-and-support}Focal quantile
sparsification (\(q = 0.8\)) sharpens ground-truth support recovery
while preserving nearly all linear subspace fidelity
(\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_benchmark}, mean \(\pm\) SE with direct line-end
labels across default-positivity fits at \texttt{q\ =\ None},
\texttt{q\ =\ 0.5}, and \texttt{q\ =\ 0.8}). (A) Out-of-sample linear
(\texttt{Ridge(XV)}) and single-axis (\texttt{RandomForest(XV)}) scores
across the 12 cohort blocks. (B) Ground-truth feature support recovery
(\(F_1\)) across the sparse synthetic regimes.}

\end{figure}%

\section{Non-Subtractive Sparsification and Two-Stage Uniform Shrinkage:
Synthetic Verification (Propositions
1--3)}\label{sec-exp-synthetic-v043}

Numerical checks and 20-seed synthetic benchmarks verify Propositions
1--3 and two-stage support-restricted uniform shrinkage
(\texttt{results\_cache/v043\_synthetic\_theorems.json} from
\texttt{scripts/benchmark\_synthetic\_theorems.py};
Section~\ref{sec-non-subtractive-sparsification};
Section~\ref{sec-uniform-shrinkage}; Donoho and Johnstone (1994)).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Proposition 1 (Subtractive Shrinkage Cancellation,
  Equation~\ref{eq-prop1-cancellation}):} Across shrinkage levels
  \(\alpha \in [0, 0.99]\) on \(p = 100\) coordinates at \(q = 0.30\),
  order-statistic quantile soft-thresholding
  (\texttt{sparseness\_alg="soft"}) yields maximum \(\ell_\infty\)
  deviation \(1.07 \times 10^{-15}\) from the \(\alpha = 0\) support and
  weights, confirming exact \(\alpha\)-cancellation. Under
  uniform-shrunk coordinate floors, continuous pre-retraction
  soft-thresholding (\texttt{sparseness\_type="soft"},
  \texttt{\_apply\_soft\_threshold\_sparseness}) suffers floor erosion:
  at \(\alpha = 0.99\), the uniform floor reaches 0.0826 above the
  threshold \(\tau =\) 0.0415, pulling cosine similarity with
  \(\alpha = 0\) down to 0.666 (Figure~\ref{fig-v043-propositions} A).
\item
  \textbf{Proposition 2 (Exact Top-\(m\) Cardinality and Radial Simplex
  Normalization, Equation~\ref{eq-prop2-radial-simplex}):} On
  \(p = 60, m = 20\), exact cardinality-constrained projection preserves
  the Cauchy--Schwarz variance bound with absolute error
  \(0.00 \times 10^{0}\) and achieves lower Euclidean squared error
  (68.53 vs.~69.24) and higher cosine similarity (0.894 vs.~0.855) than
  shifted soft-thresholding. At \texttt{sparseness=None} (\(p = 100\)),
  legacy Euclidean simplex projection subtracts \(\theta =\) 0.1285,
  zeros 81\% of active coordinates, and lowers cosine similarity with
  the input direction to 0.718, whereas radial simplex normalization
  retains cosine 1.000 with zero dropped coordinates
  (Figure~\ref{fig-v043-propositions} B).
\item
  \textbf{Proposition 3 (Column-Support Uniform Shrinkage Orthogonality,
  Equation~\ref{eq-prop3-support-shrinkage}):} On \(k = 2\) disjoint
  column supports of size {[}40, 40{]},
  \texttt{ColumnSupportShrinkageOperator} maintains exact orthogonality
  across \(\alpha \in [0, 1]\) with maximum Gram Frobenius error
  \(9.99 \times 10^{-16}\), whereas global
  \texttt{UniformShrinkageOperator} mixes the supports until Frobenius
  error reaches 1.414 and column cosine reaches 1.000 at \(\alpha = 1\)
  (Figure~\ref{fig-v043-propositions} C).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[htbp]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{03_experiments_files/figure-pdf/cell-11-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-v043-propositions}Numerical verification of
Propositions 1--3 on \texttt{pysimlr\ v0.43.1}
(\texttt{v043\_synthetic\_theorems.json}). (A) Cosine similarity with
the \(\alpha = 0\) weight vector across \(\alpha \in [0, 0.99]\)
(\(p = 100, q = 0.30\)): order-statistic quantile soft-thresholding
(\texttt{sparseness\_alg="soft"}) cancels \(\alpha\) exactly while
continuous pre-retraction soft-thresholding
(\texttt{sparseness\_type="soft"}) degrades under the uniform shrinkage
floor. (B) Comparison of exact top-\(m\) vs.~soft-threshold cosine
(\(p = 60, m = 20\)) and legacy Euclidean vs.~radial
\texttt{\_convexify} cosine/zeroing at \texttt{sparseness=None}
(\(p = 100\)). (C) Gram orthogonality Frobenius error
\(\|\mathbf{V}^\top \mathbf{V} - \mathbf{I}_2\|_F\) and inter-column
cosine across \(\alpha \in [0, 1]\) on disjoint {[}40, 40{]} supports:
\texttt{ColumnSupportShrinkageOperator} holds exact zero error while
global uniform shrinkage collapses to collinearity.}

\end{figure}%

We next evaluate two-stage support-restricted uniform shrinkage
(\texttt{fit\_uniform\_shrunk}, Donoho--Johnstone Risk Inflation
Criterion \texttt{ric} Equation~\ref{eq-ric-dof} vs.~\texttt{plugin}
shrinkage) across 20 seeds in a four-view generative setting (\(M = 4\),
view dimensions (80, 100, 60, 160), \(N_{\text{train}} =\) 60,
\(N_{\text{test}} =\) 200, block-correlated noise \(\rho = 0.45\) in
blocks of size \(5\)). Design A tests rank \(k = 1\) with 30\% active
positive signal per view (Table~\ref{tbl-v043-design-a}); Design B tests
rank \(k = 2\) with disjoint 25\%/25\% active supports per view
(Table~\ref{tbl-v043-design-b}; Figure~\ref{fig-v043-design-ab}).

conical and radial convex arms outperform global shrinkage and legacy

Across the 20 seeds in Design A (\(k = 1\), two-sided Wilcoxon
signed-rank tests), two-stage conical shrinkage at \(q = 0.5\) with
plugin \(\alpha\) achieves \(R^2 =\) 0.898 \(\pm\) 0.011 and \(F_1 =\)
0.750 \(\pm\) 0.001 against global uniform shrinkage (0.829 \(\pm\)
0.019, \(F_1 =\) 0.462 \(\pm\) 0.000; \(\Delta R^2 =\) +0.069, \(p =\)
\(1.91 \times 10^{-6}\); \(\Delta F_1 =\) +0.288, \(p =\)
\(1.19 \times 10^{-5}\)) and exceeds legacy soft-thresholding (0.880
\(\pm\) 0.014; \(\Delta R^2 =\) +0.018, \(p =\)
\(1.91 \times 10^{-6}\)). Under theoretical RIC shrinkage at
\(q = 0.5\), two-stage conical attains \(R^2 =\) 0.878 \(\pm\) 0.014 and
\(F_1 =\) 0.750 \(\pm\) 0.001 (\(\Delta R^2 =\) +0.048, \(p =\)
\(1.91 \times 10^{-6}\); \(\Delta F_1 =\) +0.288, \(p =\)
\(1.19 \times 10^{-5}\)). Two-stage radial convex (\(q = 0.5\), plugin)
reaches \(R^2 =\) 0.898 \(\pm\) 0.011 and \(F_1 =\) 0.749 \(\pm\) 0.002
against legacy Euclidean convex (0.891 \(\pm\) 0.012, \(F_1 =\) 0.744
\(\pm\) 0.033; \(\Delta R^2 =\) +0.008, \(p =\) \(4.77 \times 10^{-5}\);
\(\Delta F_1 =\) +0.005, \(p =\) \(2.16 \times 10^{-1}\)).

In Design B (\(k = 2\) disjoint supports), two-stage conical shrinkage
(\(q = 0.5\), RIC) achieves mean \(R^2 =\) 0.872 \(\pm\) 0.098 and
\(F_1 =\) 0.811 \(\pm\) 0.107 with exact orthogonality (\texttt{fro\ =}
0.0000, \texttt{cos\ =} 0.0000), outperforming global uniform shrinkage
(0.845 \(\pm\) 0.080, \(F_1 =\) 0.520 \(\pm\) 0.033, \texttt{fro\ =}
0.1617; \(\Delta R^2 =\) +0.027, \(p =\) \(7.30 \times 10^{-3}\);
\(\Delta F_1 =\) +0.290, \(p =\) \(1.91 \times 10^{-6}\);
\(\Delta \text{Fro} =\) -0.162, \(p =\) \(1.91 \times 10^{-6}\)) and
legacy conical hard thresholding (0.842 \(\pm\) 0.070, \(F_1 =\) 0.677
\(\pm\) 0.017, \texttt{fro\ =} 0.1333; \(\Delta R^2 =\) +0.030, \(p =\)
\(1.21 \times 10^{-3}\); \(\Delta F_1 =\) +0.133, \(p =\)
\(8.51 \times 10^{-4}\)). Two-stage radial convex (\(q = 0.5\), RIC)
attains \(R^2 =\) 0.885 \(\pm\) 0.036 and \(F_1 =\) 0.817 \(\pm\) 0.075
(\texttt{fro\ =} 0.0000), improving over legacy Euclidean convex (0.855
\(\pm\) 0.063, \(F_1 =\) 0.673 \(\pm\) 0.046, \texttt{fro\ =} 0.0853;
\(\Delta R^2 =\) +0.030, \(p =\) \(8.20 \times 10^{-5}\);
\(\Delta F_1 =\) +0.144, \(p =\) \(1.91 \times 10^{-6}\)).

\section{\texorpdfstring{Clinical Neuroimaging in \texttt{ADNI-MCI}
(\(N = 1,084\)): Predicting Cognition from MRI Alone and Attributing the
Prediction}{Clinical Neuroimaging in ADNI-MCI (N = 1,084): Predicting Cognition from MRI Alone and Attributing the Prediction}}\label{clinical-neuroimaging-in-adni-mci-n-1084-predicting-cognition-from-mri-alone-and-attributing-the-prediction}

A primary clinical test of multi-modal representation learning is
whether aligning structural neuroimaging with multi-domain cognitive
batteries during training yields an anatomical basis that better
predicts cognitive severity in new patients who have only undergone an
MRI scan (\texttt{Protocol\ B}). Across \(N = 1,084\) participants with
Mild Cognitive Impairment, Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-summary} compares all
seven benchmark models on held-out MRI-only prediction of composite
cognitive impairment (\texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\), after in-fold adjustment
for age, sex, and education), single-axis Random Forest prediction, and
mean ridge regression \(R^2\) across all eleven individual
neuropsychological scales (\texttt{ADAS11}, \texttt{ADAS13},
\texttt{ADASQ4}, \texttt{MMSE}, \texttt{CDRSB}, \texttt{TRABSCOR},
\texttt{FAQ}, and four \texttt{RAVLT} memory scales).

Comparing the unsupervised anatomical baselines against
cognitive-aligned SiMR models in Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-summary} and
Figures \ref{fig-adni-view-chord}--\ref{fig-adni-scores} yields four
findings:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Supervising MRI Factor Discovery with Cognitive Batteries
  Improves Out-of-Sample MRI Prediction}: Unsupervised \texttt{PCA} on
  the \(382\) structural MRI features explains \(R^2 =\) 0.148 \(\pm\)
  0.010 of out-of-sample cognitive variance (\texttt{CogPC1}) on the
  linear probe and 0.042 on the single-axis Random Forest probe.
  Aligning the MRI projections with the 11-scale cognitive battery
  during training gives, for the four leading models (3-seed means
  \(\pm\) SD), \texttt{NED} (0.209 \(\pm\) 0.027), \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}
  (0.198 \(\pm\) 0.021), \texttt{NEDPP} (0.192 \(\pm\) 0.022),
  \texttt{LEND} (0.192 \(\pm\) 0.029); the best is +41.2\% over
  \texttt{PCA}. The best single-axis Random Forest score is 0.155
  (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}), 3.7 times that of \texttt{PCA}. In the
  single-seed sweep the best configuration reaches 0.2686
  (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}) and the best linear-family configuration 0.2595
  (\texttt{SiMLR}), against 0.1869 for \texttt{PCA}; mean prediction
  across all eleven scales reaches 0.124 (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}) against
  0.085 for \texttt{PCA} (+46.0\%). These sweep peaks are maxima over
  many configurations on one split and are optimistic.
\item
  \textbf{Which MRI Features Carry the Prediction}:
  Figure~\ref{fig-adni-neuroanatomy-flagship} attributes the held-out
  \texttt{CogPC1} prediction of the non-negative \(q = 0.85\) fit (all
  nine MRI columns, \(R^2 =\) 0.170). One column per MRI view carries
  it. Alone, the predictive column gives \(R^2 =\) 0.122 in volume
  (component 1), 0.195 in vol/area (component 1) and 0.084 in DiReCT
  thickness (component 2, 1 region), against at most 0.068 for any other
  column, and only the vol/area column is not redundant with the rest
  (unique \(R^2\) +0.040). Component numbers are not identifiable across
  fits, so the refits below follow each view's predictive column rather
  than a fixed index. Across 20 half-sample refits, the predictive
  vol/area basis selects these features in at least half of them
  (fraction of refits): L entorhinal (DKT parcellation): 0.95; L
  temporal lobe: 0.90; L inferior parietal (DKT cortex label): 0.85; L
  entorhinal (DKT cortex label): 0.80; R entorhinal (DKT cortex label):
  0.80; R entorhinal (DKT parcellation): 0.75. These are mostly
  entorhinal, temporal-lobe and inferior parietal measures, the regions
  where atrophy is expected in prodromal Alzheimer's disease ({Dickerson
  et al.} 2009; {Jack et al.} 2018). DiReCT thickness tells the same
  story less clearly. Its first principal component explains 58\% of the
  variance across the 62 regions (mean inter-region correlation 0.55),
  and consensus component 1 correlates 0.87 with subject-mean thickness,
  so hard-assigning each region to its largest structure coefficient
  places 62 / 0 / 0 regions on components 1 / 2 / 3: a display of the
  global factor, not of localisation (Panel B). The thickness basis
  itself is unstable under the default collapse penalty: it keeps two or
  fewer regions in 8 of 20 refits (median held-out \(R^2\) 0.118).
  Non-negative region sums on positively correlated features typically
  correlate above the penalty's threshold of 0.3, so the penalty pushes
  those columns towards one or two regions. With
  \texttt{collapse\_weight\ =\ 0} the predictive thickness basis keeps a
  median of 9 regions (two or fewer in 1 refit), its median \(R^2\)
  rises to 0.144, and its most frequent regions are (fraction of refits)
  L entorhinal: 0.55; L supramarginal: 0.45 (Panel D). No single
  thickness region replicates in more than 0.55 of refits, so the
  thickness map is a pattern, not a list of identified regions
  (\texttt{docs/audit/evidence/adni\_global\_factor\_check.csv}). For
  reference, unthresholded non-negative fits are 67\% sparse on the DKT
  view without rotation and 67\% with Varimax, against 0\% for dense
  \texttt{PCA}.
\item
  \textbf{Support-Shuffled and PCA-Rotated Score Controls}: To separate
  coordinate-level axis alignment and anatomical support localization
  from subspace capacity, two negative controls are evaluated across 3
  seeds on \texttt{ADNI-MCI}
  (\texttt{adni\_mci\_axis\_alignment\_controls.csv},
  \texttt{axis\_alignment\_control\_metrics}): (i) a PCA-rotated score
  control rotates the learned scores
  \(\mathbf{Z} = [\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m]\) by their in-fold
  principal-component eigenvectors, which leaves the linear probe
  unchanged (largest difference \(1.08 \times 10^{-7}\)) and lowers the
  single-axis Random Forest score for 2 of 5 models; (ii) a
  support-shuffled basis control permutes the rows of each
  \(\mathbf{V}_m\) within its view, preserving sparsity, weight
  magnitudes and \(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m\), and lowers the
  Random Forest \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) for 5 of 5 models:
  \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} (0.151 \(\to\) 0.091), \texttt{LEND} (0.119
  \(\to\) 0.059), \texttt{NEDPP} (0.134 \(\to\) 0.096),
  \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} (0.033 \(\to\) -0.059), \texttt{SiMLR} (0.094
  \(\to\) 0.028). Unshuffled Random Forest scores are 1.0 to 4.8 times
  that of \texttt{PCA}.
\item
  \textbf{Rank Selection, Component Identifiability, and Display
  Policy}: Evaluating latent rank selection via
  \texttt{select\_rank(...,\ criterion="latent\_reliability")}
  (Section~\ref{sec-stability-bagging-sem}; Equation~\ref{eq-oob-icc1})
  across 20 half-sample refits
  (\texttt{docs/audit/evidence/adni\_rank\_selection.csv}, selection
  margin 0.2) selects \(k =\) 2 under non-negative estimation
  (\texttt{positivity="positive"}) and \(k =\) 2 under signed estimation
  (\texttt{positivity="either"}). At \(k = 2\) under non-negativity,
  out-of-bag univariate latent reliabilities are 0.891 and 0.975 (gains
  0.438 and 0.630), multivariate reliability is 0.945 (gain 0.520), and
  \texttt{all\_coupled\ =} True (\texttt{passed\ =} True). At \(k = 3\)
  under non-negativity, univariate reliabilities are 0.928, 0.502, and
  0.920 (gains 0.516, 0.219, and 0.652): Component 2 drops to minimum
  reliability 0.502 (gain 0.219), multivariate reliability drops to
  0.790 (gain declines from 0.520 at \(k = 2\) to 0.402, failing
  multivariate monotonicity), and \texttt{all\_coupled\ =} False
  (\texttt{passed\ =} False), so the fixed \(k = 3\) diagnostic fits
  below isolate Components 1 and 3 as the two high-reliability axes.
  Under signed estimation (\texttt{positivity="either"}), \(k = 2\)
  achieves reliabilities 0.697 and 0.603 (multivariate gain 0.456,
  \texttt{passed\ =} True), whereas at \(k = 3\) reliabilities drop to
  0.882, 0.645, and 0.197 (minimum gain -0.053, multivariate gain 0.362,
  \texttt{passed\ =} False).

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Signed Displays Protocol}: Across all displays in this
    manuscript: (i) feature maps display only features whose 95\%
    bootstrap confidence interval (from whole-pipeline refits in
    \texttt{loading\_inference}) strictly excludes zero; (ii) named
    biomarker panels report features selected via Meinshausen--Bühlmann
    stability selection at threshold \(\pi_{\text{thr}} = 0.80\) with
    expected false selections \(E[\text{false}] \le 1.0\); and (iii)
    non-negative displays present hard-assignment OLEE loadings
    accompanied by the \texttt{loading\_margin} certainty metric.
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

loadings \(\mathbf{L}_m\) across \texttt{Heart} and \texttt{Diabetes}
modalities.

In \texttt{MultiOmics} (\texttt{TCGA-BRCA}, \(N =\) 822), out-of-sample
Estrogen Receptor classification accuracy on the linear entrypoint is
92.5\% \(\pm\) 1.2\% (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}), 89.9\% \(\pm\) 1.2\%
(\texttt{LEND}) and 92.2\% \(\pm\) 1.3\% (\texttt{NEDPP}), against
91.5\% \(\pm\) 1.8\% for \texttt{PCA} (3-seed means \(\pm\) SD); the
sparse non-negative dictionaries \(\mathbf{V}_m\), tri-modal feature
chord, and view-level quality radar are examined in
Appendix~\ref{sec-app-brca}. In \texttt{TCGA-KIRC} (\(N = 467\)),
pathologic stage classification on the consensus embedding
\(\mathbf{U}\) is 64.1\% \(\pm\) 1.1\% for \texttt{LEND} against 67.1\%
\(\pm\) 1.5\% for \texttt{PCA}. On compact clinical cohorts,
\texttt{Diabetes} (\(N =\) 442) reaches \(R^2 =\) 0.352 for
\texttt{SiMLR} against 0.351 for \texttt{PCA}, and \texttt{Heart}
(\(N =\) 297) reaches accuracy 0.848 for \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} and 0.819
for \texttt{SiMLR}. Figure~\ref{fig-diabetes-top-features},
Figure~\ref{fig-heart-audit}, and
Figure~\ref{fig-nsa-v-interpretability} show the stability-selected
structure correlations, multi-view feature-to-component chord, and OLEE
loadings \(\mathbf{L}_m\) across \texttt{Heart} (out-of-bag
\texttt{latent\_reliability} 0.73 and 0.57) and \texttt{Diabetes}
(\texttt{latent\_reliability} 0.95 and 0.83); while the linear
entrypoint \(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\)
maps individual observations into the latent consensus space, the OLEE
loadings \(\mathbf{L}_m\) decompose individual patient profiles into
exact additive risk attributions \(c_{m,i,j,l} = x_{m,i,j} l_{m,j,l}\)
without post-hoc Monte Carlo approximations (Wachter et al. 2018; Karimi
et al. 2021; Pearl 2009). Complete parameter sensitivity ablations (the
\(w \in [0.01, 0.95]\) trade-off curve, basis diagnostics, stability
rank selection across \(k\), and synthetic noise recovery) are in
Appendix~\ref{sec-app-operational}.

\section{Re-Benchmarking on Six Biomedical Cohorts (v0.43.1):
Non-Subtractive Sparsification, Radial Simplex, and Uniform
Shrinkage}\label{sec-exp-v043-revisit}

To measure the empirical impact of Propositions 1--3 on real biomedical
cohorts (\texttt{pysimlr\ v0.43.1},
\texttt{scripts/run\_real\_data\_v043.py}), we re-benchmark standard
\texttt{simlr} (\texttt{Part\ A}) and evaluate two-stage uniform
shrinkage \texttt{fit\_uniform\_shrunk} (\texttt{Part\ B}) across all
six real cohorts (\texttt{ADNI-MCI}, \texttt{MultiOmics}
{[}\texttt{TCGA-BRCA}{]}, \texttt{TCGA-KIRC}, \texttt{Heart},
\texttt{Diabetes}, \texttt{mfeat}) over three random seeds
(\texttt{42,\ 43,\ 44}).

In Part A (\texttt{part\_a\_comparison\_summary.csv},
Table~\ref{tbl-v043-part-a}), 107 of 108 \texttt{simlr} fits (99.1\%)
converge; the single non-convergent run is \texttt{TCGA-KIRC} under
\texttt{positivity="either"}, \texttt{sparseness=None}, seed \texttt{44}
(\texttt{Stop\_Reason\ =\ "max\_iter"}). Across the 81 matched fits (27
cohort-by-positivity-by-sparseness cells \(\times\) 3 seeds) comparing
\texttt{v0.43.1} against the pre-\texttt{v0.43} cache, signed
\texttt{positivity="either"} improves mean consensus metric by +0.007
(+0.003 at \(q = 0.5\) and +0.019 at \(q = 0.8\)), while non-negative
\texttt{positivity="positive"} ranges from -0.026 to +0.007
(\(\max |\Delta| =\) 0.026). Under radial simplex normalization
(\texttt{positivity="convex"}) on the three high-dimensional biomedical
cohorts, \texttt{MultiOmics} (\texttt{TCGA-BRCA}) reaches peak consensus
accuracy 0.894 and mean linear accuracy 0.916 (mean consensus 0.890),
ahead of \texttt{positivity="positive"} (peak consensus 0.870, mean
linear 0.895) and \texttt{positivity="either"} (peak consensus 0.836,
mean linear 0.824).

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 16\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1154}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 16\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1346}}

In Part B (\texttt{part\_b\_uniform\_shrunk\_summary.csv},
Table~\ref{tbl-v043-part-b}; Figure~\ref{fig-v043-real-revisit}), we
evaluate 96 \texttt{fit\_uniform\_shrunk} runs across all six cohorts
and ranks \(k \in \{1, 2, 3\}\) (capped at \(k \in \{1, 2\}\) for
\texttt{Heart} and \texttt{Diabetes}). Under
\texttt{positivity="conical"}, 48 of 48 fits converge with maximum Gram
defect \(D(\mathbf{V}_m) \le\) \(3.79 \times 10^{-14}\) and maximum
inter-column cosine 0.000. Under radial simplex
\texttt{positivity="convex"}, 45 of 48 fits converge with maximum
inter-column cosine 0.000 and maximum Gram defect 0.482 (non-zero defect
arises because radial \(\ell_1\)-simplex columns have unequal \(\ell_2\)
norms when disjoint support sizes differ). The three non-converged
\texttt{convex} fits are all on \texttt{Diabetes} (\(p = [5, 5]\)) at
\(k = 2\) (\texttt{Stop\_Reason\ =\ "no\_descent"} across seeds
\texttt{42,\ 43,\ 44}, consensus 0.273, linear 0.353), whereas
\texttt{positivity="conical"} converges in all \(3/3\) splits with
consensus 0.331, linear 0.405, and Gram defect \(1.78 \times 10^{-15}\).
Pooled across cohorts for \texttt{conical}, mean consensus score rises
from 0.463 (\(k = 1\), linear 0.524) to 0.530 (\(k = 2\), linear 0.641)
and 0.572 (\(k = 3\), linear 0.681). At each cohort's target rank
(\(k = 2\) for \texttt{Heart} and \texttt{Diabetes}, \(k = 3\) for
\texttt{ADNI-MCI}, \texttt{MultiOmics}, \texttt{TCGA-KIRC}, and
\texttt{mfeat}), \texttt{fit\_uniform\_shrunk} (\texttt{conical})
reaches linear scores of 0.925 (\texttt{MultiOmics}), 0.931
(\texttt{mfeat}), 0.830 (\texttt{Heart}), 0.662 (\texttt{TCGA-KIRC}),
0.405 (\texttt{Diabetes}), and 0.209 (\texttt{ADNI-MCI}).

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Discussion and Practical
Guidelines}\label{discussion-and-practical-guidelines}

\section{Synthesizing Clinical Neurobiology and Statistical
Representation
Learning}\label{synthesizing-clinical-neurobiology-and-statistical-representation-learning}

The empirical results across \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, \texttt{TCGA-BRCA},
\texttt{TCGA-KIRC}, and the synthetic ground-truth benchmarks
demonstrate that predictive accuracy and mechanistic localization do not
need to be traded against one another when multi-view integration is
organized around an explicit linear entrypoint
(\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\)). For the
clinical neuroscientist or neuropsychologist, the primary value of
\texttt{SiMR} lies in how cross-modal supervision shapes the learned
anatomical basis. When structural MRI is decomposed in isolation via
\texttt{PCA}, every one of the \(62\) DKT parcels loads on every axis,
and the result explains \(R^2 =\) 0.148 of composite cognitive severity
(\texttt{CogPC1}), falling to 0.042 under single-axis decision tree
splits. Coupling structural MRI views to an 11-scale cognitive battery
during training (\texttt{Protocol\ B}) with non-negative approximate
Stiefel regularization (\texttt{nsa\_flow}, \(w = 0.50, q = 0.85\))
concentrates the held-out prediction in one component per view (vol/area
\(R^2 =\) 0.195 alone), whose replicated features are entorhinal,
temporal-lobe and inferior parietal measures
(Figure~\ref{fig-adni-neuroanatomy-flagship}). Whether this pattern
corresponds to stages of neurofibrillary pathology (Braak and Braak
1991; {Dickerson et al.} 2009; {Jack et al.} 2018) is not tested here.
On DiReCT cortical thickness one consensus component tracks a global
factor (58\% of the variance), and its regional basis replicates only
when the collapse penalty is turned off
(\texttt{collapse\_weight\ =\ 0}).

For the machine learning researcher, our four-probe evaluation resolves
why post-hoc rotation or unmixing steps (\texttt{ICA} (Hyvärinen and Oja
2000), orthogonal Procrustes rotations (Schönemann 1966), or dense SVD
factors) often appear competitive under ridge regression yet fail when
individual coordinates are inspected or thresholded. Because
\(\operatorname{span}(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m \mathbf{M}) = \operatorname{span}(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m)\)
for any invertible \(\mathbf{M} \in \mathrm{GL}(k)\), linear subspace
probes measure only aggregate subspace capacity. Pairing linear subspace
probes with coordinate-aligned Random Forest probes (\texttt{RF(XV)})
and ground-truth support recovery (\(F_1\)) exposes diagonal coordinate
mixing directly: across all 12 benchmarks, the Random Forest minus
linear score is -0.044 for \texttt{PCA}, against \texttt{SiMLR}
(-0.017), \texttt{NEDPP} (-0.020) for the models with the smallest drop,
and support recovery on the \(50\%\)-sparse \texttt{Linear} regime
ranges from \(F_1 =\) 0.500 to 1.000.

\section{Practical Method and Operating-Point Selection
Guide}\label{practical-method-and-operating-point-selection-guide}

To translate these findings into actionable guidance for new multi-modal
studies, Table~\ref{tbl-practitioner-guide} maps common biomedical study
designs to the recommended \texttt{SiMR} solver and quantile sparsity
operating point \(q\).

Multi-View Clinical Translation (e.g., training on MRI + Cognition,
deploying on MRI alone; \(N \sim 10^3\)) & \texttt{LEND} or
\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} & \(w = 0.50\), \(q = 0.50\) (discovery) or
\(q = 0.85\) (focal) & On \texttt{ADNI-MCI} MRI-only cognitive
prediction the best 3-seed linear score is 0.209 (\texttt{NED}) and the
best single-axis score 0.155 (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}); at \(q = 0.85\) one
component carries the prediction; on views dominated by a global factor
set \texttt{collapse\_weight\ =\ 0} before reading regional loadings. \\
High-Dimensional Multi-Omics Pathway Discovery (e.g., mRNA + CNV + RPPA;
\(p_m \gg N\)) & \texttt{SiMLR} or \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} & \(w = 0.50\),
\(q = 0.50\), \texttt{positivity="positive"} & \texttt{TCGA-BRCA}
accuracy 0.802 (\texttt{SiMLR}); support recovery on
\texttt{NonnegParts} \(F_1 =\) 0.579. \\
Minimal Diagnostic Assay / Targeted Biomarker Panel Design &
\texttt{SiMLR} & \(w = 0.50\), \(q = 0.80\)--\(0.85\),
\texttt{positivity="positive"} & At \(q = 0.8\), 57.8\% of loadings are
zero while 99.8\% of the best mean linear score is retained (best
\(F_1\) on the \(80\%\)-sparse \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak} regime
0.831). \\
Non-Linear Cross-Assay Coupling (e.g., renal cancer stage across
heterogeneous assays) & \texttt{LEND} & \(w = 0.50\), \(q = 0.50\) &
Non-linear decoders with linear input projections
\(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) (\texttt{TCGA-KIRC} consensus accuracy
64.1\% \(\pm\) 1.1\% vs.~67.1\% \(\pm\) 1.5\% for \texttt{PCA}). \\
Low-Dimensional Clinical Tabular Vitals (\(p_m < 15\), homogeneous
noise) & \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} or \texttt{SiMLR} & \(w = 0.50\),
\(q = 0.50\) & Exact patient-level clinical attribution
(\(c_{ij} = x_{ij} l_{jl}\) from OLEE loadings; \texttt{Heart} accuracy
0.848 for \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA}, \texttt{Diabetes} \(R^2 =\) 0.352 for
\texttt{SiMLR}). \\
Raw Abundance / Compositional / Dietary Intake Tables (where the
Perron-Frobenius global mean mode \emph{is} the target signal) &
\texttt{SiMLR} (\texttt{varimax=False}) & \(w = 0.50\),
\texttt{varimax=False} (\texttt{init="pca\_clamp"}) & Preserves the
leading all-positive global abundance/caloric mode on Component 1 rather
than rotating it across all \(k\) axes. \\
Sign-Oriented Directional Views or \(n_m \lesssim p_m\) &
\texttt{fit\_uniform\_shrunk} + \texttt{resolve\_directional\_defaults}
& \texttt{positivity="auto"}, \texttt{alpha\_mode="ric"},
\(q \in [0.3, 0.5]\) (or \texttt{positivity="conical"} when
\(p_m \le 5\)) & Exact column-support orthogonality
(\(D(\mathbf{V}_m) \le\) \(3.79 \times 10^{-14}\)), Design B support
recovery \(F_1 =\) 0.811 and radial convex \(R^2 =\) 0.885, and
\texttt{TCGA-BRCA} conical linear probe 0.925
(Section~\ref{sec-uniform-shrinkage};
Section~\ref{sec-exp-v043-revisit}). \\
Subsample-Aggregated Discovery \& Prediction & \texttt{bagged\_fit} +
\texttt{bagged\_predict} & \texttt{aggregate="select"} (sparse support)
or \texttt{"average"} (Procrustes-aligned refits),
\texttt{n\_subsamples=50}, \texttt{fraction=0.8}, \texttt{threshold=0.6}
& Stabilizes high-dimensional feature supports across subsamples while
\texttt{latent\_stability} (\texttt{select\_rank}) quantifies out-of-bag
\texttt{latent\_reliability}
(Section~\ref{sec-stability-bagging-sem}). \\
Downstream Pathway \& Mediation Graphs & \texttt{pysimlr.path\_model}
vs.~\texttt{observed\_sem} (\texttt{observed\_path\_model}) &
\texttt{path\_model} for multi-column blocks;
\texttt{observed\_path\_model} /
\texttt{compare\_observed\_path\_models} for single-variable Gaussian
paths & Separates distribution-free permutation Pillai block tests from
\texttt{observed\_sem} single-variable ML SEM fit indices (\(\chi^2\),
RMSEA, CFI, SRMR, AIC/BIC; Section~\ref{sec-stability-bagging-sem}; Hu
and Bentler (1999); Steiger (1990); Akaike (1974); Schwarz (1978)). \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Limitations and Negative
Findings}\label{limitations-and-negative-findings}

The regimes where \texttt{SiMR} architectures underperform, incur
computational trade-offs, or require specific preprocessing are:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{No Universal Advantage Over PCA on Unaligned Single-View
  Unsupervised Tasks}: When a single homogeneous data matrix is analyzed
  without cross-modal supervision or when views share identical
  signal-to-noise ratios and dense Gaussian loadings, standard
  \texttt{PCA} maximizes variance explanation in
  \(\mathcal{O}(\min(N p^2, p N^2))\) time (median 0.010 s here). On
  low-dimensional clinical tables such as \texttt{Heart}
  (\(p = [7, 6]\)), \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} reaches 0.848 against 0.819 for
  multi-view \texttt{SiMLR}.
\item
  \textbf{Unrotated Initialization and Row-Mean Centering on Cortical
  Thickness}: On \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, non-negative \texttt{SiMLR} without
  rotation or quantile thresholding
  (\texttt{varimax\ =\ False,\ sparseness\ =\ None}) is 67\% sparse with
  {[}60, 2, 0{]} active DKT regions per component; with Varimax rotation
  it is 67\% sparse with {[}2, 0, 60{]}. Subject row-mean centering of
  the MRI views (Appendix~\ref{sec-app-operational}; the cognitive
  outcome is not row-centered) gives 56\% sparsity and \texttt{CogPC1}
  \(R^2 =\) 0.150 (PCA start) or 0.171 (ICA start), against 0.208 with
  column centering. Sparsity alone does not make the thickness basis
  interpretable: at \(q = 0.85\) the components keep {[}62, 0, 0{]}
  regions, because the collapse penalty reduces columns on a globally
  correlated view to one or two regions, and the regional pattern
  appears only with \texttt{collapse\_weight\ =\ 0}
  (Figure~\ref{fig-adni-neuroanatomy-flagship} D).
\item
  \textbf{Extreme Orthogonality Weights}: Moving the \texttt{nsa\_flow}
  weight from \(w = 0.50\) to \(w = 0.95\) multiplies fit time by 5.0 on
  \texttt{ADNI-MCI} and 3.8 on synthetic \texttt{NonnegParts}, and
  changes \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) from 0.218 to 0.211. At \(w = 0.01\)
  the effective rank is 3.00 of \(k = 3\) (Gram defect \(D =\) 0.000);
  at \(w = 0.10\) it is 3.00. Across \(w \in [0.20, 0.85]\),
  \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) stays within 0.205--0.218.
\item
  \textbf{Deep Multi-View Models and Feature Support Recovery}: On the
  synthetic parts regimes, the deep autoencoder variants recover feature
  supports less well than the linear solver: \(F_1 =\) 0.753
  (\texttt{NED}) and 0.720 (\texttt{NEDPP}) on \texttt{NonnegParts}
  against 0.579 for \texttt{SiMLR}, and 0.475 and 0.443 on
  \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak} against 0.644.
\item
  \textbf{Dimensional Collapse vs.~Correlated Factor Trade-Off
  (\texttt{collapse\_threshold\ =\ 0.3})}: Preventing dimensional
  collapse is essential in narrow or unbalanced views. The default
  penalty (\texttt{collapse\_weight\ =\ 1.0},
  \texttt{collapse\_threshold\ =\ 0.3};
  \texttt{docs/audit/evidence/collapse\_threshold\_selection.csv})
  protects narrow views from collapsing into duplicate factors (at
  \(t = 0.7\), narrow-view score correlation reaches 0.89 and outcome
  \(R^2\) changes by -0.10 (from 0.9582 to 0.8561)), at the trade-off of
  capping recoverable score correlation between correlated latent
  factors near \(t = 0.3\) under average mixing (\texttt{avg}: 0.302 at
  \(\rho = 0.5\) and 0.309 at \(\rho = 0.7\)), whereas Newton polar
  mixing decorrelates consensus scores to 0.000.
\item
  \textbf{Unified Budgets and Optimizer Convergence Basins}:
  Optimization uses a unified default budget of \(300\) linear sweeps
  and \(300\) deep epochs with plateau tolerance
  \texttt{DEFAULT\_TOL\ =\ 1e-4}
  (\texttt{docs/audit/evidence/default\_selection\_stage1.csv}, 1,664
  fits; \texttt{default\_selection\_stage2.csv}, 180 fits with 1.000
  convergence and worst-case \(R^2 =\) 0.9759;
  \texttt{deep\_default\_consistency.csv}, 18 runs per family with
  convergence rates 1.000 for \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}, 0.889 for
  \texttt{LEND}, 0.944 for \texttt{NED}, and 0.611 for \texttt{NEDPP}).
  Dimensional collapse in unpenalized linear and deep fits is a
  step-size optimization basin rather than an objective minimum:
  unpenalized deep fits that collapse incur worse similarity loss than
  uncollapsed fits (mean -1.48, \(n =\) 7, versus -1.94, \(n =\) 13;
  \texttt{docs/audit/evidence/deep\_collapse\_diagnosis.csv}), and
  short-horizon step-size probes favor upper grid endpoints, motivating
  the 7-point probe grid spanning \texttt{1.0} to \texttt{0.001}.
\item
  \textbf{Scale-Free View Weighting and Standardization Invariance}:
  Equal view weights \(\alpha_m = 1/M\) preserve scale invariance for
  normalized correlation and related scale-free energies, preventing
  view-weight spreads from muting the unweighted collapse penalty, while
  standardized energies (\texttt{align}, \texttt{logcosh}, \texttt{exp},
  \texttt{kurtosis}, \texttt{negentropy}) use a scale-relative variance
  floor so rescaling any view leaves the objective invariant.
\item
  \textbf{Polarity User Contract and Mixed-Sign Cancellation}:
  \texttt{fit\_uniform\_shrunk} requires caller-aligned feature signs
  (for example via \texttt{prepare\_views}); unaligned opposing signs
  cancel under the within-support mean projector \(\Pi_m\), and
  non-negative constraints suppress negative loadings
  (Section~\ref{sec-uniform-shrinkage}).
\item
  \textbf{Radial Simplex Non-Convergence on Very Low-Dimensional Views
  (\(p_m \le 5\))}: Under \(k = 2\) disjoint support extraction
  (\texttt{min\_per\_col=2}), radial simplex faces
  (\texttt{positivity="convex"}) failed line-search descent on
  \texttt{Diabetes} (\(0/3\) converged, \texttt{no\_descent}, consensus
  0.273, linear 0.353), whereas \texttt{positivity="conical"} converged
  in \(3/3\) splits (consensus 0.331, linear 0.405, \(D =\)
  \(1.78 \times 10^{-15}\); Section~\ref{sec-exp-v043-revisit}).
\item
  \textbf{Disjoint Support Assumption at \(k > 1\)}: In
  \texttt{ColumnSupportShrinkageOperator}
  (Equation~\ref{eq-prop3-support-shrinkage}), exact zero-defect
  orthogonality requires pairwise-disjoint Stage-1 lobe supports;
  settings requiring shared pleiotropic hubs across components at
  \(k > 1\) should use standard \texttt{SiMLR} with \texttt{nsa\_flow}
  (\(w = 0.50\)).
\end{enumerate}

\cleardoublepage

\appendix

\chapter{Multi-Omics Clinical Validation
(TCGA-BRCA)}\label{sec-app-brca}

\section{Multi-Omic Integration
Strategy}\label{multi-omic-integration-strategy}

We evaluate the seven benchmark architectures on the three-view
\texttt{TCGA-BRCA} breast invasive carcinoma cohort (\(N =\) 822 primary
tumors with complete multi-omic profiles and clinical receptor status)
(The Cancer Genome Atlas Network 2012; {Perou et al.} 2000; {Parker et
al.} 2009; {Curtis et al.} 2012; Weinstein et al. 2013). The dataset
integrates three molecular assay platforms filtered at \(\ge 98\%\)
completeness:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{RNA-Seq (\texttt{rna}):} \(p_1 =\) 200 top-variance mRNA
  sequencing gene expression transcripts (Illumina HiSeqV2).
\item
  \textbf{Copy Number Variation (\texttt{cnv}):} \(p_2 =\) 150 somatic
  GISTIC2 gene-level copy-number scores.
\item
  \textbf{Proteomics (\texttt{rppa}):} \(p_3 =\) 150 Reverse Phase
  Protein Array targeted proteomic and phosphoproteomic markers.
\end{itemize}

The downstream clinical target is binary Estrogen Receptor (ER) status
(Positive vs.~Negative, 76.4\% ER+ prevalence), evaluated across 3
independent \(70/30\) stratified train/test splits
(\texttt{Seeds\ 42,\ 43,\ 44}, standardized strictly inside each
training fold).

\section{Performance Results}\label{performance-results}

Table~\ref{tbl-brca-appendix-results} reports out-of-sample ER status
classification accuracy across both primary evaluation probes: global
consensus coordinates (\(\mathbf{U}\)) and the linear entrypoint
projection (\(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\)), alongside Stiefel frame
defect \(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\), sparsity, and execution runtime computed
directly from
\texttt{paper/results\_cache/deep\_ranking\_benchmark.csv}. On the
linear entrypoint probe, \texttt{NED} reaches 0.929 against 0.915 for
\texttt{PCA} (consensus 0.922), while \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} reaches
92.5\% \(\pm\) 1.2\%, \texttt{LEND} reaches 89.9\% \(\pm\) 1.2\%, and
\texttt{SiMLR} reaches 0.802.

\section{Tri-Modal Molecular Driver Recovery and Multi-View
Fingerprints}\label{tri-modal-molecular-driver-recovery-and-multi-view-fingerprints}

The linear entrypoint maps the 500 multi-omic features directly onto the
low-dimensional latent coordinates, while
\texttt{pysimlr.loading\_inference} refits the full pipeline across 100
half-sample splits and 20 cross-view permutations to separate
reproducible drivers from in-sample selection noise. On the ER-aligned
component C3 (\(|r| =\) 0.67 with ER status), out-of-bag
\texttt{latent\_reliability} is 0.78 (multivariate reliability 0.79),
held-out \texttt{cross\_view\_coupling} is 0.64, and overall
\texttt{feature\_agreement} is 0.86 (against a feature-structure
permutation null maximum \(|r| \le\) 0.82).
Figure~\ref{fig-brca-trimodal-waterfall} displays
\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_top\_features} on component C3 learned by
\texttt{LEND} across the three molecular modalities
(\texttt{brca\_er\_loadings.json} from
\texttt{scripts/paper\_brca\_loadings.py}),
Figure~\ref{fig-brca-feature-chord} maps the stability-selected and
interval-significant biomarkers across all three views onto components
\texttt{C1–C3} via \texttt{pysimlr.plot\_feature\_chord}, and
Figure~\ref{fig-brca-view-radar} summarizes each assay's training and
held-out coupling, held-out reconstruction \(R^2\), normalized effective
rank, and sparsity via \texttt{pysimlr.plot\_view\_radar}.

\chapter{Extended Parameter Ablations and Operational
Details}\label{sec-app-operational}

This appendix provides the complete empirical ablation tables referenced
in the main text, covering the \texttt{nsa\_flow} trade-off weight
\(w \in [0.01, 0.95]\), in-fold subject row-mean removal and
deterministic ICA initialization, fine-grained quantile sparsity
\(q \in [\text{None}, 0.90]\), the 1,025-configuration \texttt{ADNI-MCI}
grid sweep, synthetic per-regime breakdowns, and Wilcoxon signed-rank
tests against \texttt{PCA}, followed by computational complexity bounds.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Effect of the Approximate Stiefel Weight
\(w \in [0.01, 0.95]\)}{Effect of the Approximate Stiefel Weight w \textbackslash in {[}0.01, 0.95{]}}}\label{effect-of-the-approximate-stiefel-weight-w-in-0.01-0.95}

\texttt{SiMLR} (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0, k = 3\)) across ten values of
\(w \in [0.01, 0.95]\) on synthetic \texttt{NonnegParts} and clinical
\texttt{ADNI-MCI} (\texttt{Protocol\ B}). Across \(w \in [0.20, 0.85]\),
\texttt{ADNI-MCI} \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) spans 0.205 to 0.218 (0.211 at
\(w = 0.01\), 0.218 at \(w = 0.50\), and 0.211 at \(w = 0.95\)), while
effective rank reaches 3.00 at \(w = 0.01\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Basis Health and Rank-Selection Stability
Diagnostics on
\texttt{ADNI-MCI}}{Basis Health and Rank-Selection Stability Diagnostics on ADNI-MCI}}\label{basis-health-and-rank-selection-stability-diagnostics-on-adni-mci}

To verify optimization conditioning and component identifiability
directly on the four-view \texttt{ADNI-MCI} cohort
(\texttt{Protocol\ B}), Figure~\ref{fig-adni-basis-diagnostics} applies
\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_basis\_diagnostics} (\(k = 3\), \(q = 0.85\)),
reporting per-view effective rank against \(k\), maximum column cosine
overlap, \(\log_{10}\) condition number, and achieved sparsity alongside
the median \texttt{loading\_margin}.
Figure~\ref{fig-adni-rank-selection} applies
\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_rank\_selection}
(\texttt{criterion="latent\_reliability"},
Section~\ref{sec-stability-bagging-sem} and Equation~\ref{eq-oob-icc1})
to the 20-subsample \texttt{select\_rank} evidence
(\texttt{docs/audit/evidence/adni\_rank\_selection.csv}), requiring
every component's out-of-bag \texttt{latent\_reliability\_gain}
(\(\mathrm{ICC}(1) - \text{baseline}\)) to clear 0.2, non-decreasing
multivariate reliability gain, and \texttt{all\_coupled\ =\ True}. Under
\texttt{positivity="positive"}, \texttt{select\_rank} selects \(k =\) 2:
at \(k = 2\), component reliabilities are 0.891 and 0.975 (gains 0.438
and 0.630, multivariate reliability 0.945, gain 0.520,
\texttt{all\_coupled\ =} True); at \(k = 3\), Component 2 drops to 0.502
(gain 0.219), multivariate gain falls to 0.402 (failing multivariate
monotonicity), and \texttt{all\_coupled\ =} False (\texttt{passed\ =}
False). Under signed estimation (\texttt{positivity="either"}),
\texttt{select\_rank} also selects \(k =\) 2: at \(k = 2\), component
reliabilities are 0.697 and 0.603 (gains 0.478 and 0.432, multivariate
reliability 0.651, gain 0.456, \texttt{all\_coupled\ =} True), whereas
at \(k = 3\) Component 3 drops to 0.197 (gain -0.053, \texttt{passed\ =}
False).

\begin{figure}[htbp]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{appendix_operational_files/figure-pdf/fig-adni-basis-diagnostics-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-adni-basis-diagnostics}Non-negative sparse
\texttt{ADNI-MCI} bases achieve full effective rank, low column overlap,
well-conditioned Gram spectra, and target sparsity across all four
modalities (\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_basis\_diagnostics},
\texttt{Protocol\ B}, \texttt{SiMLR}, \(k = 3\),
\texttt{positivity=\textquotesingle{}positive\textquotesingle{}},
\(q = 0.85\)). Panels plot per-view effective and numerical rank against
\(k = 3\), maximum column overlap, \(\log_{10}\) condition number,
achieved sparsity against \(0.85\), and median OLEE loading margins.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{figure}[htbp]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{appendix_operational_files/figure-pdf/fig-adni-rank-selection-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-adni-rank-selection}Out-of-bag latent reliability
rank selection identifies \(k = 2\) for both non-negative and signed
\texttt{ADNI-MCI} fits (\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_rank\_selection},
\texttt{criterion=\textquotesingle{}latent\_reliability\textquotesingle{}},
20 half-sample refits from
\texttt{docs/audit/evidence/adni\_rank\_selection.csv}). Vertical range
stems and per-component dots plot \texttt{latent\_reliability\_gain}
(\(\text{ICC}(1) - \text{baseline}\)) with weakest-component and median
traces, dashed lines mark the \texttt{0.2} selection margin, and square
markers plot multivariate reliability gain across
\(k \in \{2, 3, 4, 5\}\); non-negative estimation achieves higher
multivariate reliability at \(k = 2\), at \(k = 3\) \texttt{positive}
clears the \texttt{0.2} gain threshold (minimum gain \texttt{0.2191})
while failing multivariate monotonicity and cross-view coupling whereas
\texttt{either} fails both gain and monotonicity, and both regimes fail
both the \texttt{0.2} gain and monotonicity gates for \(k \ge 4\).}

\end{figure}%

\section{Preprocessing Controls: Subject Row-Mean Removal, ICA
Initialization, and Orthogonal Varimax
Rotation}\label{preprocessing-controls-subject-row-mean-removal-ica-initialization-and-orthogonal-varimax-rotation}

On neuroimaging cohorts where cortical regions share a positive global
correlation floor, Table~\ref{tbl-app-rowmean-ica} and
Figure~\ref{fig-rowmean-k-ablation} compare column centering with
in-fold subject row-mean centering, PCA with deterministic FastICA
(Hyvärinen and Oja 2000) initialization, signed with non-negative bases,
and the initializer alone with the fitted model (\texttt{SiMLR},
\(k = 3\), \(w = 0.50\), no Varimax, no quantile threshold;
\texttt{scripts/paper\_adni\_ablations.py}). Row-mean centering applies
to the three MRI views only; the eleven cognitive scales are on
different units, and subtracting each subject's mean across them would
remove overall severity from the outcome. With non-negative bases,
row-mean centering changes structural MRI sparsity from 92\% to 56\% and
\texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) from 0.208 to 0.150 with PCA initialization (and
to 54\% sparsity and 0.171 \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) with ICA
initialization). Varimax rotation on column-centered data gives 67\%
sparsity on the 62 DKT regions with {[}2, 0, 60{]} active regions per
component, against 67\% and {[}60, 2, 0{]} without rotation.

\chapter{Normalizing Flow SiMR with Linear Encoder Bottleneck
(Flow-SiMR-V)}\label{sec-app-flow}

\section{Model Formulation}\label{model-formulation}

\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} prepends a linear entrypoint projection matrix
\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\) to each view's invertible
normalizing flow (Dinh et al. 2017; Durkan et al. 2019; Rezende and
Mohamed 2015; Kingma and Dhariwal 2018; Papamakarios et al. 2021;
Kobyzev et al. 2020). This architecture provides a linear bottleneck at
the input boundary while retaining bijective density mapping,
conditional cross-view inference, and latent trajectory interpolation on
the \(k\)-dimensional subspace. Under \texttt{flow\_type="auto"}
(default), \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} dispatches to a coordinate-wise strictly
monotone rational-quadratic spline flow with positive data-dependent
ActNorm (\texttt{ActNormElementwiseRQSplineFlow} (Durkan et al. 2019))
when \(k = 1\)---where RealNVP has an empty conditioner partition
(\texttt{split\_dim\ =\ 0}) and reduces to a global affine map---and to
Real-valued Non-Volume Preserving (\texttt{RealNVP}) affine coupling
layers (Dinh et al. 2017) when \(k \ge 2\), with optional post-warmup
Hadamard Principal Component Flow (\texttt{pcf\_alpha}) Jacobian
row-orthogonality regularization (Cunningham et al. 2022). The flows are
implemented in PyTorch ({Paszke et al.} 2019), adapting coupling
structures from the Latent-Aligned Multiview Normalizing Flows
(\texttt{LAMNrFlows}) framework
(\href{https://github.com/ntustison/LAMNrFlows}{ntustison/LAMNrFlows}).

\subsection{Mathematical Formulation}\label{mathematical-formulation}

For each input modality \(\mathbf{X}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times p_m}\)
(\(m = 1, \dots, M\)):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Linear entrypoint bottleneck:} \[
  \mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m, \qquad \mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k},
  \] where \(\mathbf{V}_m\) is regularized via \texttt{nsa\_flow}
  (\(w \in (0, 1)\), default \(w = 0.5\)) or polar retraction (Higham
  2008; Absil et al. 2008).
\item
  \textbf{Low-dimensional bijective normalizing flow:} \[
  \mathbf{Z}_m = f_{\text{flow}, m}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}),
  \] where \(f_{\text{flow}, m}: \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^k\) is a
  monotone rational-quadratic spline bijection (Durkan et al. 2019) at
  \(k = 1\) (or when \texttt{flow\_type="spline1d"}) and a composition
  of RealNVP affine coupling layers (Dinh et al. 2017) at \(k \ge 2\),
  with exact inverse \(f_{\text{flow}, m}^{-1}\) and tractable
  log-determinant
  \(\log |\det J_{f_{\text{flow}, m}}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}})|\).
\item
  \textbf{Consensus mixing and inverse reconstruction:} \[
  \mathbf{U}_m = \operatorname{Consensus}\big(\{\mathbf{Z}_j\}_{j \in \mathcal{N}(m)}\big), \qquad \hat{\mathbf{X}}_m = f_{\text{flow}, m}^{-1}(\mathbf{U}_m) \mathbf{V}_m^\top.
  \]
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Rectangular OLEE Prior Collapse and Detached-Prior
Optimization}\label{rectangular-olee-prior-collapse-and-detached-prior-optimization}

A structural distinction separates square ambient-space normalizing
flows (\texttt{Flow-SiMR}, where
\(f_{\text{flow},m}: \mathbb{R}^{p_m} \to \mathbb{R}^{p_m}\), stabilized
with bounded coupling scales \texttt{scale\_bound\ =\ 0.5}, optional
Probabilistic PCA base distributions
\texttt{base\_distribution="gaussian\_pca"}, and exact
likelihood-preserving post-flow shared \(\mathrm{O}(k)\) Varimax gauge
rotations \texttt{varimax=True} (Kaiser 1958)) from rectangular
OLEE-bottlenecked flows (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}, where
\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\) with \(k \ll p_m\)
precedes \(f_{\text{flow},m}: \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^k\)).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Why End-to-End Gaussian Prior Gradients
Collapse
\(\mathbf{V}_m\)}{Why End-to-End Gaussian Prior Gradients Collapse \textbackslash mathbf\{V\}\_m}}\label{why-end-to-end-gaussian-prior-gradients-collapse-mathbfv_m}

In standard normalizing flows, maximum likelihood training minimizes the
negative log-likelihood under a standard isotropic Gaussian prior
\(p(\mathbf{z}) = \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, \mathbf{I}_k)\): \[
\mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}; f_{\text{flow},m}) = \frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^n \|f_{\text{flow},m}(\mathbf{z}_{m,\text{lin},i})\|_2^2 - \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \log \left|\det \frac{\partial f_{\text{flow},m}}{\partial \mathbf{z}_{m,\text{lin}}}(\mathbf{z}_{m,\text{lin},i})\right|.
\] When the rectangular projection
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\)
(\(k \ll p_m\)) precedes the flow, the change-of-variables determinant
\(\log |\det J_{f_{\text{flow},m}}|\) accounts only for volume changes
inside the \(k\)-dimensional subspace and contains no log-determinant
term for the rectangular projection \(\mathbf{V}_m\). If
\(\nabla_{\mathbf{V}_m} \mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}\) is backpropagated
into \(\mathbf{V}_m\), the quadratic penalty
\(\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{Z}_m\|_2^2 = \frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m) + \mathcal{O}(\|f_{\text{flow},m} - \mathrm{id}\|)\)
is minimized by rotating \(\mathbf{V}_m\) onto the trailing eigenvectors
of \(\mathbf{X}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m\) with the smallest eigenvalues
\(\lambda_{p_m-k+1}, \dots, \lambda_{p_m}\).

\subsection{Detached-Prior Subspace-Decoupled
Optimization}\label{detached-prior-subspace-decoupled-optimization}

To prevent rectangular prior collapse, \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} detaches
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}\) at the input to the Gaussian prior
penalty:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{OLEE basis update (\(\mathbf{V}_m\)):} Updated exclusively by
  the cross-view alignment energy \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{align}}\) and
  within-view relative reconstruction loss
  \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{recon}}\), while detaching
  \(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}\) in the prior term
  (\(\operatorname{sg}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}) = \mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}.\text{detach}()\)).
\item
  \textbf{Bijective flow update (\(f_{\text{flow},m}\)):} Trained via
  \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}(f_{\text{flow},m}(\operatorname{sg}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}))) + \mathcal{L}_{\text{align}} + \mathcal{L}_{\text{recon}}\),
  initialized with zero scale and translation weights
  (\(s_\ell = 0, t_\ell = 0\)) at epoch \(0\) so
  \(f_{\text{flow},m}^{(0)}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}) = \mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}\).
\end{enumerate}

In the \texttt{ADNI-MCI} hyperparameter sweep (\(N = 1,084\), \(k = 3\),
\texttt{Protocol\ B}, 1,025 configurations), the best
\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} configuration reaches an out-of-sample
\texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) of 0.2686; across the 3-seed benchmark its
linear score on \texttt{ADNI-MCI} is 0.198, and its mean Friedman rank
on the linear probe over all 12 datasets is 3.58.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Performance and Diagnostic
Visualizations}\label{performance-and-diagnostic-visualizations}

\subsection{Conditional Clinical Imputation (True vs.~Imputed
Serums)}\label{conditional-clinical-imputation-true-vs.-imputed-serums}

Because \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} preserves invertibility on the
\(k\)-dimensional bottleneck space, it supports conditional cross-view
inference via the Gaussian Schur complement in post-flow latent space
(\texttt{pysimlr.predict\_conditional\_view}). In
Figure~\ref{fig-flow-imputation}, we train \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} on the
Diabetes dataset under a leak-free 70/30 split, withhold the blood serum
view on test patients, and evaluate serum triglyceride (\texttt{s5})
imputation from vitals alone via \texttt{pysimlr.plot\_predictions}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{appendix_flow_simr_v_files/figure-pdf/fig-flow-imputation-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-flow-imputation}Flow-SiMR-V conditional
Schur-complement decoding (\texttt{pysimlr.predict\_conditional\_view})
imputes held-out serum triglycerides (\texttt{s5}) from systemic vitals
alone (\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_predictions}). Points show observed versus
imputed training-standardized \texttt{s5} on the 30\% held-out Diabetes
test split (\(k = 2\)), with the solid line marking identity and the
header reporting test correlation and error.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Flow Warping, Stacked Term Convergence, and Dynamic Modality
Weights}\label{flow-warping-stacked-term-convergence-and-dynamic-modality-weights}

To audit how the downstream normalizing flow transforms the linear
first-layer projection \(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m \mathbf{R}\) into the
aligned latent coordinates \(\mathbf{Z}_m \mathbf{R}\),
Figure~\ref{fig-flow-warping} applies
\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_flow\_warping} on \texttt{Diabetes} (\(k = 2\),
\texttt{dynamic\_weights=True}): monotone binned median traces and high
Spearman \(\rho\) confirm that the coupling layers bend densities
smoothly without folding or inverting the sign semantics of
\(\mathbf{V}_m\). Figure~\ref{fig-flow-convergence} and
Figure~\ref{fig-flow-weights} display the corresponding stacked
objective-term trajectories (\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_convergence},
separating total loss, cross-view similarity, and reconstruction onto
independent scales without secondary axes) and
degree-of-freedom-corrected MAI view weights
(\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_modality\_weights}).

\begin{figure}[htbp]

\centering{

}

layers preserve the directional sign ordering of the linear entrypoint
on the two-view Diabetes cohort (\texttt{pysimlr.plot\_flow\_warping},
\texttt{flow\_v}, \(k = 2\)). Scatter plots compare the first-layer
linear projection \((\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m \mathbf{R})_j\)
(horizontal) against the post-flow latent coordinate
\((\mathbf{Z}_m \mathbf{R})_j\) (vertical), with monotone binned median

interpretability across downstream normalizing flow coupling layers.

\subsection{Latent Trajectory
Interpolation}\label{latent-trajectory-interpolation}

Tracing a straight-line interpolation in the shared latent space between
a low-risk and high-risk patient and inverting it through
\(f_{\text{flow},m}^{-1}\) and \(\mathbf{V}_m^\top\) defines a smooth
trajectory in feature space (Figure~\ref{fig-flow-geodesic}).

\begin{figure}[htbp]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{appendix_flow_simr_v_files/figure-pdf/fig-flow-geodesic-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-flow-geodesic}Invertible flow geodesics trace
continuous biomarker evolution curves between low-risk and high-risk
clinical phenotypes. Decoded biomarker trajectories along a
straight-line latent interpolation path between Patient \#0 (low-risk,
\(\alpha = 0.0\)) and Patient \#10 (high-risk, \(\alpha = 1.0\)) on the
Diabetes cohort (\(k = 2\)). Colored trajectories track Body Mass Index
(\texttt{bmi}, blue circles), Blood Pressure (\texttt{bp}, orange
squares), and Age (\texttt{age}, green triangles) decoded through
\(f_{\text{flow},0}^{-1}\) and \(\mathbf{V}_0^\top\).}

\end{figure}%

\chapter{Reproducibility and Statistical
Methodology}\label{sec-app-repro}

This appendix documents the computational environment, replication
structure, and non-parametric statistical testing procedures used to
generate all tables, figures, and hypothesis tests in the manuscript
(\texttt{pysimlr\ v0.43.1}).

\section{Building the Manuscript}\label{building-the-manuscript}

The manuscript is written in Quarto and compiles to HTML and PDF
formats. All benchmark result caches are stored in
\texttt{paper/results\_cache/} and \texttt{docs/audit/evidence/}, each
written by a committed script under \texttt{scripts/} and accompanied by
provenance metadata. Every number in the text is computed from these
caches at build time (\texttt{paper/paper\_numbers.py}), so the
manuscript is rebuilt deterministically from them:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{git}\NormalTok{ clone https://github.com/stnava/pysimlr.git}
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ pysimlr}
\ExtensionTok{pip}\NormalTok{ install }\AttributeTok{{-}e} \StringTok{".[dev]"}
\ExtensionTok{quarto}\NormalTok{ render paper}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

All eight \texttt{v0.43.1} compute scripts expose a \texttt{-\/-smoke}
flag (\texttt{counts=2}, \texttt{\textless{}\ 10} s wall-clock runtime,
non-destructive temporary output paths when \texttt{-\/-out} /
\texttt{-\/-output} / \texttt{-\/-out-dir} is omitted, and
\texttt{"smoke":\ true} recorded in the provenance JSON):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{scripts/run\_deep\_benchmark.py} (\texttt{-\/-seeds\ 3},
  \texttt{-\/-workers\ 8}, \texttt{-\/-iterations\ N},
  \texttt{-\/-epochs\ N}, \texttt{-\/-suite}, \texttt{-\/-consistency},
  \texttt{-\/-out\ CSV}, \texttt{-\/-out-dir\ DIR}, \texttt{-\/-smoke}):
  writes \texttt{deep\_ranking\_benchmark.csv} and its sparsity splits
  (\texttt{deep\_ranking\_benchmark\_q\_none.csv},
  \texttt{deep\_ranking\_benchmark\_q05.csv}, and
  \texttt{deep\_ranking\_benchmark\_q08.csv}), along with
  \texttt{operating\_points\_05\_08\_benchmark.csv},
  \texttt{provenance\_deep\_benchmark.json}, and
  \texttt{deep\_default\_consistency.csv} in
  \texttt{paper/results\_cache/}.
\item
  \texttt{scripts/run\_real\_data\_v043.py} (\texttt{-\/-out\ DIR},
  \texttt{-\/-workers\ 8}, \texttt{-\/-n-seeds\ 3},
  \texttt{-\/-iters\ N}, \texttt{-\/-reuse-existing},
  \texttt{-\/-skip-part-c}, \texttt{-\/-part-b-only},
  \texttt{-\/-smoke}): writes \texttt{part\_a\_simlr\_v043\_raw.csv} and
  \texttt{part\_a\_comparison\_summary.csv}, along with
  \texttt{part\_b\_uniform\_shrunk\_raw.csv},
  \texttt{part\_b\_uniform\_shrunk\_summary.csv}, and the provenance
  metadata \texttt{provenance\_v043\_real\_data.json} in
  \texttt{paper/results\_cache/}.
\item
  \texttt{scripts/benchmark\_synthetic\_theorems.py}
  (\texttt{-\/-n-seeds\ 20}, \texttt{-\/-n-jobs\ 8},
  \texttt{-\/-output\ JSON}, \texttt{-\/-smoke}): writes
  \texttt{v043\_synthetic\_theorems.json} in
  \texttt{paper/results\_cache/}.
\item
  \texttt{scripts/paper\_adni\_ablations.py} (\texttt{-\/-out\ DIR},
  \texttt{-\/-only\ \{controls,\ k,\ loadings,\ quantile,\ rowmean,\ w\}},
  \texttt{-\/-workers\ 8}, \texttt{-\/-smoke}): writes the ablation
  tables \texttt{ablation\_w\_sweep.csv} and
  \texttt{ablation\_k\_sparsity\_sweep.csv}, plus the quantile table
  \texttt{ablation\_quantile\_sparseness.csv} and the row-mean table
  \texttt{ablation\_rowmean\_ica\_positivity.csv}, as well as
  \texttt{adni\_mci\_axis\_alignment\_controls.csv},
  \texttt{adni\_mci\_feature\_loadings.json}, and
  \texttt{provenance\_adni\_ablations.json} in
  \texttt{paper/results\_cache/}.
\item
  \texttt{scripts/paper\_adni\_attribution.py} (\texttt{-\/-out\ JSON},
  \texttt{-\/-smoke}): writes \texttt{adni\_attribution.json} in
  \texttt{paper/results\_cache/}.
\item
  \texttt{scripts/adni\_rank\_selection.py} (\texttt{-\/-out\ CSV},
  \texttt{-\/-ks\ 2\ 3\ 4\ 5}, \texttt{-\/-n-subsamples\ 20},
  \texttt{-\/-n-baseline\ 3}, \texttt{-\/-iterations\ N},
  \texttt{-\/-n-jobs\ 8}, \texttt{-\/-smoke}) produces
  \texttt{docs/audit/evidence/adni\_rank\_selection.csv} and
  \texttt{adni\_rank\_selection\_provenance.json}.
\item
  \texttt{scripts/paper\_brca\_loadings.py} (\texttt{-\/-out\ DIR},
  \texttt{-\/-n-subsamples\ 100}, \texttt{-\/-n-permutations\ 20},
  \texttt{-\/-iterations\ N}, \texttt{-\/-n-jobs\ 8},
  \texttt{-\/-smoke}) produces \texttt{brca\_er\_loadings.json}.
\item
  \texttt{scripts/paper\_nsa\_v\_loadings.py} (\texttt{-\/-out\ DIR},
  \texttt{-\/-n-subsamples\ 50}, \texttt{-\/-n-permutations\ 20},
  \texttt{-\/-iterations\ N}, \texttt{-\/-n-jobs\ 8},
  \texttt{-\/-smoke}) produces \texttt{nsa\_v\_loadings.json}.
\end{itemize}

The single-seed \texttt{ADNI-MCI} hyperparameter sweep
(\texttt{scripts/run\_adni\_mci\_grid\_sweep.py}) is preserved unchanged
(\texttt{adni\_mci\_1214\_grid\_sweep.csv}, 1,025 of 1,025
configurations completed; provenance recorded in
\texttt{provenance\_adni\_mci\_grid\_sweep.json}).

\section{Non-Parametric Benchmark Engine and Optimization
Stack}\label{non-parametric-benchmark-engine-and-optimization-stack}

All statistical hypothesis tests, ranking matrices, and permutation null
distributions are computed by
\texttt{src/pysimlr/benchmarks/deep\_ranking.py}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Non-parametric Friedman omnibus test (Friedman 1937) and
  Iman--Davenport \(F\)-distribution extension (Demšar 2006; Iman and
  Davenport 1980).
\item
  Two-tailed Nemenyi Critical Difference (\(CD =\) 2.600 at
  \(\alpha = 0.05\) for \(k =\) 7, \(B =\) 12) (Nemenyi 1963; Demšar
  2006).
\item
  Pairwise Wilcoxon signed-rank tests (Wilcoxon 1945) with
  Holm--Bonferroni step-down adjustments (Holm 1979).
\item
  Adaptive first-order (\texttt{Adam}, \texttt{AdamW} (Kingma and Ba
  2015; Loshchilov and Hutter 2019)) and Riemannian manifold solvers
  implemented on top of PyTorch ({Paszke et al.} 2019), NumPy ({Harris
  et al.} 2020), SciPy ({Virtanen et al.} 2020), and Scikit-Learn
  ({Pedregosa et al.} 2011).
\item
  Benchmark cohorts span clinical cardiology (\texttt{Heart} (Detrano et
  al. 1989)), metabolic progression (\texttt{Diabetes} (Efron et al.
  2004)), handwritten pattern descriptors (\texttt{mfeat} (Breukelen et
  al. 1998)), transcriptomic/multi-omic cancer benchmarks ({Golub et
  al.} 1999; The Cancer Genome Atlas Network 2012; The Cancer Genome
  Atlas Research Network 2013; Wang et al. 2014; Tenenhaus and Tenenhaus
  2011; Tenenhaus et al. 2014; Zhao et al. 2017; Abdi et al. 2013), and
  Alzheimer's neuroimaging (\texttt{ADNI-MCI} (Mueller et al. 2005;
  {Jack et al.} 2008; {Weiner et al.} 2017)).
\end{itemize}

The test suites \texttt{tests/test\_deep\_ranking\_stats.py} and
\texttt{tests/test\_implementation\_standards.py} verify the statistical
calculations and package standards:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{pytest}\NormalTok{ tests/test\_deep\_ranking\_stats.py tests/test\_implementation\_standards.py }\AttributeTok{{-}v}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Block-Level Sign-Flip Permutation
Floor}\label{block-level-sign-flip-permutation-floor}

In paired comparisons across \(B =\) 12 independent dataset blocks
(where each block cell is the mean across \(N_{\text{seeds}} =\) 3
replicates (Demšar 2006)), the exact distribution-free sign-flip null
distribution across the \(B =\) 12 blocks consists of \(2^B = 4,096\)
sign assignments:

\[
p_{\text{perm}} = \frac{1}{2^B} \sum_{\mathbf{s} \in \{-1, +1\}^B} \mathbb{I}\left(\left|\frac{1}{B} \sum_{i=1}^B s_i \Delta_i\right| \ge |\bar{\Delta}|\right), \qquad p_{\min, B=12} = \frac{2}{2^{12}} = \frac{2}{4096} \approx 0.000488
\]

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{v0.43.1} Replication Designs and
Provenance
Ledger}{v0.43.1 Replication Designs and Provenance Ledger}}\label{v0.43.1-replication-designs-and-provenance-ledger}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Synthetic Propositions and Design A/B Benchmark
  (\texttt{v043\_synthetic\_theorems.json}):}
  \texttt{scripts/benchmark\_synthetic\_theorems.py} evaluates 20 random
  seeds (\(N_{\text{train}} =\) 60, \(N_{\text{test}} =\) 200, view
  dimensions (80, 100, 60, 160)) with paired two-sided Wilcoxon
  signed-rank tests across arms.
\item
  \textbf{Six-Cohort Real-Data Revisit (\texttt{part\_a\_*},
  \texttt{part\_b\_*}):} \texttt{scripts/run\_real\_data\_v043.py}
  evaluates Part A (108 \texttt{simlr} fits across 6 real cohorts
  \(\times\) 3 seeds, yielding 81 matched comparisons across 27
  pre-\texttt{v0.43} cells in \texttt{part\_a\_simlr\_v043\_raw.csv} and
  \texttt{part\_a\_comparison\_summary.csv}) and Part B (96
  \texttt{fit\_uniform\_shrunk} fits: 48 conical and 48 radial-convex
  across 6 cohorts \(\times k \in \{1, 2, 3\} \times\) 3 seeds in
  \texttt{part\_b\_uniform\_shrunk\_raw.csv} and
  \texttt{part\_b\_uniform\_shrunk\_summary.csv}).
\item
  \textbf{Audit Evidence and Provenance Ledger
  (\texttt{docs/audit/evidence/}):} Supporting design evidence tables
  record 20-subsample rank selection
  (\texttt{adni\_rank\_selection.csv}), two-stage default selection
  (\texttt{default\_selection\_stage1.csv}, 1,664 fits;
  \texttt{default\_selection\_stage2.csv}, 180 fits across 20 cells),
  scale-free view weighting (\texttt{view\_weighting\_acc.csv}),
  collapse threshold selection
  (\texttt{collapse\_threshold\_selection.csv}), unpenalized deep
  collapse (\texttt{deep\_collapse\_diagnosis.csv}), and deep
  convergence consistency (\texttt{deep\_default\_consistency.csv}, 18
  runs per family). All \texttt{v0.43.1} benchmark and audit caches were
  generated on \texttt{2026-11-19} (\texttt{Darwin\ arm64},
  \texttt{torch\ 2.13.0}, \texttt{smoke=false}), while the
  1,025-configuration \texttt{ADNI-MCI} grid sweep
  (\texttt{adni\_mci\_1214\_grid\_sweep.csv}) is preserved from
  \texttt{2026-11-05} (\texttt{pysimlr\ 0.20.0},
  \texttt{provenance\_adni\_mci\_grid\_sweep.json},
  \texttt{n\_failed=0}).
\end{itemize}
